[0:00 - 0:06] In 1980, Peter Vig published a seminal study titled Experimental Manipulation of Head Posture.
[0:06 - 0:11] Dr. Vig wanted to understand what triggers shifts in natural head position,
[0:11 - 0:16] specifically looking at how breathing influences head posture.
[0:16 - 0:24] He took some students and artificially blocked their nasal passages using nose clips to achieve total nasal obstruction.
[0:25 - 0:31] Of course, this forced the subjects to instantly switch from nasal breathing to mouth breathing.
[0:31 - 0:40] The study found that total nasal obstruction resulted in an immediate and consequent extension of the head.
[0:40 - 0:45] The heads tipped upwards and then pushed forwards in all cases.
[0:45 - 0:52] The body naturally changes its head posture to widen the pharyngeal airway space,
[0:52 - 0:54] making it physically easier to draw air.
[0:55 - 1:07] Dr. Vig's work showed a direct physiological link between compromised nasal breathing and what is widely known today as forward head posture.

[0:00 - 0:03] By the age of five, your upper jaw is already 80% grown.
[0:03 - 0:06] The rest of your body isn't even close.
[0:06 - 0:09] That gap is the whole story.
[0:09 - 0:12] Our body doesn't grow and mature all at once.
[0:12 - 0:18] It follows certain blueprints, one of which is the cephalocordial gradient,
[0:18 - 0:22] which simply means growth happens from the head downward.
[0:22 - 0:25] It applies to your face as well.
[0:25 - 0:31] At the very top is the neurocranium, the bony vault that protects your brain.
[0:31 - 0:36] At birth, the neurocranium is already 25% of its adult size,
[0:36 - 0:40] while the face is relatively tiny and flat.
[0:40 - 0:46] The lower facial bones, like the maxilla and the mandible, develop comparatively later.
[0:46 - 0:49] So your maxilla comes next.
[0:49 - 0:50] That is your upper jaw.
[0:50 - 0:55] It forms the roof of your mouth and the floor of your nose and much of your midface.
[0:55 - 1:02] By the age of five, it is already about 80 to 85% of its adult size.
[1:02 - 1:07] From there, growth slows, complete by the mid to late teens.
[1:07 - 1:12] Suspended at the bottom of your face is your mandible, the lower jaw,
[1:12 - 1:14] which develops on a later timetable.
[1:14 - 1:20] Between the ages of five and ten, it grows more slowly than the upper jaw,
[1:20 - 1:25] which is why so many children naturally appear to have a recessed jaw.
[1:26 - 1:33] It keeps growing into the late teens and structural changes can carry on into your mid-twenties.
[1:33 - 1:40] In short, the cephalocordial gradient explains why the skull is top-heavy in development.
[1:40 - 1:44] The brain and its bony housing are prioritized early,
[1:44 - 1:49] with the upper and lower structures maturing progressively later.
[1:49 - 1:51] Look at that order again.
[1:51 - 1:54] The maxilla shapes your midface and your airway,
[1:54 - 1:55] and it looks like this.
[1:55 - 1:59] It locks in first, while you are still a child.
[1:59 - 2:03] By the time you start caring about your jaw,
[2:03 - 2:08] the central bone that shapes your face is done growing.
[2:08 - 2:13] If you're older, the foundation is set, but you are not stuck.
[2:13 - 2:18] You can open your airway, retrain your posture,
[2:18 - 2:21] and strengthen the muscles that hold it all together.
[2:21 - 2:25] For real structural change, the tools exist,
[2:25 - 2:28] and they keep on getting better.

[0:00 - 0:02] Why is the back third of the tongue important in mewing?
[0:02 - 0:07] The back third of the tongue is made of smooth muscle, similar to the tissues in your lungs.
[0:07 - 0:13] This means they're involved in making subconscious movements, like breathing in and out.
[0:13 - 0:15] In mewing, we take advantage of this.
